People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thank you. I'm glad I finally made it in. The traffic on I-95 was just unbelievable today, I swear they've been doing construction on that same stretch for five years. My husband used to say they were just moving the same pile of dirt back and forth, and honestly, I think he was right. We used to take that route to go down to the shore every summer... anyway, I'm here because, well, I guess what finally scared me into making this appointment is that I’ve been having these thoughts lately. Just... thoughts like, 'If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.' I mean, I don’t want to hurt myself or anything, I've got my grandbabies to think about, and the store—I manage a little boutique downtown, and the girls there would be completely lost without me if I didn't show up to do the scheduling. But yeah, just those passing thoughts about not waking up. It just sort of spooked me.

It just sort of spooked me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... this whole low, flat feeling, where I just can't seem to muster up the energy for anything, it's been going on for about six months now. Which is a shame, really, because six months ago was right when the weather was starting to get nice. I usually love that time of year. I'd been taking these wonderful watercolor classes at the community center—my instructor, Mrs. Gable, she always said I had such a good eye for color. We were working on landscapes, and I had this beautiful piece started of the old lighthouse down by the cove. But I just... stopped going. And my morning walks, too. I used to walk the neighborhood every single morning, rain or shine. Mrs. Higgins next door used to set her watch by me, she'd wave from her porch with her little Yorkie. But these last six months, even those things just stopped feeling restorative. I just couldn't find the point in them anymore. It's like someone just turned the color down on everything, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in them altogether?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. That's just it. Like with my art. I still set out my paints on the kitchen table—I have this lovely little setup by the window where the morning light comes in just perfectly. It's the same window where my cat, Barnaby, likes to sun himself. He's a rescue, you know, a big orange tabby. The shelter said he was unadoptable because he was too shy, but he just needed a quiet house. Anyway, I'll set out the paints, or I'll think about going to that pottery class I signed up for at the rec center, but when I get there, or when I sit down at the table, I just... stare at the materials. I just look at them and I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s just blank. Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. Oh, goodness, yes. Way more than two weeks. Like I was saying earlier, this whole low, flat stretch has been going on for about six months now. Every single day. It's just... persistent. Which is why I completely dropped out of my usual routines, like the art classes and those morning walks I told you about. We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.

Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: So far, you've mentioned a loss of interest in activities, low energy, and thoughts about not waking up. Have you also experienced a period lasting most of the day, nearly every day, where you felt persistently down or hopeless, separate from just feeling tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. They have these lovely floral prints—tulips and daffodils, mostly yellows and soft pinks. I was telling my assistant manager, Sarah, that we should do a whole window display around a garden theme. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I think he finally picked one out that her sister helped him find. But anyway, yes, that heavy feeling is just there. It just sits on my chest all day, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two weeks where these feelings were present nearly every day, without much relief, and were definitely worse than just feeling stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an "online presence." She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.

Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.

The shelter said he was unadoptable because he was too shy, but he just needed a quiet house. Anyway, I'll set out the paints, or I'll think about going to that pottery class I signed up for at the rec center, but when I get there, or when I sit down at the table, I just... stare at the materials. I just look at them and I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s just blank. Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: So far, you've mentioned a loss of interest in activities, low energy, and thoughts about not waking up. Have you also experienced a period lasting most of the day, nearly every day, where you felt persistently down or hopeless, separate from just feeling tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. They have these lovely floral prints—tulips and daffodils, mostly yellows and soft pinks. I was telling my assistant manager, Sarah, that we should do a whole window display around a garden theme. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I think he finally picked one out that her sister helped him find. But anyway, yes, that heavy feeling is just there. It just sits on my chest all day, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two weeks where these feelings were present nearly every day, without much relief, and were definitely worse than just feeling stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. Way more than two weeks. Like I was saying earlier, this whole low, flat stretch has been going on for about six months now. Every single day. It's just... persistent. Which is why I completely dropped out of my usual routines, like the art classes and those morning walks I told you about. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period lasting several weeks or more in the past, before this current six-month stretch, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble finding joy in your usual activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, several weeks... I mean, maybe? I'm trying to think back. When I was in my forties, maybe? It's so hard to separate feeling down from just being overwhelmed with life, you know? Like when my sister moved to Florida. That was a really tough adjustment. She moved to Naples, right near the water. Have you ever been to Naples? It's beautiful, but the humidity is just awful in the summer. She has this lovely little condo with a lanai—that's what they call a screened-in porch down there—and she grows orchids on it. She actually sent me an orchid for my birthday last year, a purple one. I tried so hard to keep it alive. I read all the instructions, bought the special fertilizer, only gave it three ice cubes of water a week just like the tag said. But the leaves still turned yellow and it just completely died. I felt terrible about it. I just don't have a green thumb, I guess. My mother, though, she could grow anything. She had this huge vegetable garden when I was growing up... tomatoes, zucchini, cucumbers. We'd have fresh salads all summer long. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, feeling down for a few weeks. Honestly, I really can't say for sure if I've ever had a period like that before this whole six-month thing started. Nothing really stands out as being this bad for that long.

routines like art classes and morning walks stopped feeling restorative.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several weeks or more in the past, before this current six-month stretch, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble finding joy in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, several weeks... I mean, maybe? I'm trying to think back. When I was in my forties, maybe? It's so hard to separate feeling down from just being overwhelmed with life, you know? Like when my sister moved to Florida. That was a really tough adjustment. She moved to Naples, right near the water. Have you ever been to Naples? It's beautiful, but the humidity is just awful in the summer. She has this lovely little condo with a lanai—that's what they call a screened-in porch down there—and she grows orchids on it. She actually sent me an orchid for my birthday last year, a purple one. I tried so hard to keep it alive. I read all the instructions, bought the special fertilizer, only gave it three ice cubes of water a week just like the tag said. But the leaves still turned yellow and it just completely died. I felt terrible about it. I just don't have a green thumb, I guess. My mother, though, she could grow anything. She had this huge vegetable garden when I was growing up... tomatoes, zucchini, cucumbers. We'd have fresh salads all summer long. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, feeling down for a few weeks. Honestly, I really can't say for sure if I've ever had a period like that before this whole six-month thing started. Nothing really stands out as being this bad for that long.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for several weeks or more, even if it wasn't as long or as severe as this time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Several weeks... you know, I'm really trying to rack my brain here, but no, I honestly don't think so. I mean, the only thing that lasts several weeks that completely drains my joy is our annual inventory at the shop. Oh, my goodness, talk about losing interest in things! By the second week of counting every single pair of socks and tiny little silver earring, you just want to pull your hair out. We use these little handheld scanners now, which are supposed to make it all so much easier, but half the time the battery dies or the barcode is smudged. 

Just last year, Sarah actually dropped one of the scanners right into a bucket of mop water. The night cleaning crew had left it out—which they are absolutely not supposed to do, I've told them a hundred times, it's a terrible slipping hazard. If a customer slipped, corporate would just have a field day with us. They are always looking for an excuse to cut our store hours anyway. They sent down this new regional manager last month, this young guy in a very shiny suit, who kept pacing around my floor talking about "synergy" and "foot traffic metrics." I just looked at him and thought, you wouldn't know how to sell a greeting card if your life depended on it. 

But... oh, I'm sorry, you were asking about feeling down. No, really, I can't remember ever feeling depressed or losing my joy for a few weeks before all this started. It really just seems to be this one long stretch lately.

This rules out F33.9 (recurrent depressive disorder, unspecified) as there is no history of prior episodes. The diagnosis remains narrowed to either F32.1 (mild), F32.2 (moderate), or F33.1 (recurrent depression, current mild), F33.2 (recurrent depression, current moderate). Differentiation between F32 and F33 still depends on whether this is a single or recurrent disorder, which remains unclear. Further clarification regarding the current severity may be needed.",
  "is_final": false
}

So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A couple of weeks... no, I really don't think so. I've been trying to think back, but honestly, nothing comes to mind. Well, actually, a couple of weeks is exactly how long my husband and I went to Italy for our 25th anniversary. Oh, it was just magnificent. We started in Rome, and the architecture... you just can't even imagine it until you're standing right there looking at the Colosseum. We had this tour guide, Lorenzo, who was just so passionate about the history. He waved his hands around the whole time he talked. 

We took the train up to Florence after that, and let me tell you, the gelato there is dangerous. I must have had a cone every single day. Stracciatella was my favorite—it's like chocolate chip, but the chocolate is in these fine little ribbons instead of chunks. I tried to find something similar at the grocery store when we got back, but that brand they sell in the plastic tubs just isn't the same. The lid on those tubs always cracks down the side anyway, and then the ice cream gets freezer burn. I ended up having to throw out a whole half-gallon of butter pecan last week because it tasted like ice crystals. My husband was so disappointed, butter pecan is his absolute favorite. He likes to put a little drizzle of caramel syrup on it... 

But anyway, no, to answer your question, I really haven't ever had a couple of weeks where I felt down or lost interest in things before this whole current thing started.

My husband used to say they were just moving the same pile of dirt back and forth, and honestly, I think he was right. Just... thoughts like, 'If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.' I mean, I don’t want to hurt myself or anything, I've got my grandbabies to think about, and the store—I manage a little boutique downtown, and the girls there would be completely lost without me if I didn't show up to do the scheduling. But yeah, just those passing thoughts about not waking up. It just sort of spooked me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... this whole low, flat feeling, where I just can't seem to muster up the energy for anything, it's been going on for about six months now. Which is a shame, really, because six months ago was right when the weather was starting to get nice. I usually love that time of year. I'd been taking these wonderful watercolor classes at the community center—my instructor, Mrs. Gable, she always said I had such a good eye for color. We were working on landscapes, and I had this beautiful piece started of the old lighthouse down by the cove. But I just... stopped going. And my morning walks, too. I used to walk the neighborhood every single morning, rain or shine. Mrs. Higgins next door used to set her watch by me, she'd wave from her porch with her little Yorkie. But these last six months, even those things just stopped feeling restorative. I just couldn't find the point in them anymore. It's like someone just turned the color down on everything, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in them altogether?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. That's just it. Like with my art. I still set out my paints on the kitchen table—I have this lovely little setup by the window where the morning light comes in just perfectly. It's the same window where my cat, Barnaby, likes to sun himself. He's a rescue, you know, a big orange tabby. The shelter said he was unadoptable because he was too shy, but he just needed a quiet house. Anyway, I'll set out the paints, or I'll think about going to that pottery class I signed up for at the rec center, but when I get there, or when I sit down at the table, I just... stare at the materials. I just look at them and I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s just blank. Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: So far, you've mentioned a loss of interest in activities, low energy, and thoughts about not waking up. Have you also experienced a period lasting most of the day, nearly every day, where you felt persistently down or hopeless, separate from just feeling tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. They have these lovely floral prints—tulips and daffodils, mostly yellows and soft pinks. I was telling my assistant manager, Sarah, that we should do a whole window display around a garden theme. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I think he finally picked one out that her sister helped him find. But anyway, yes, that heavy feeling is just there. It just sits on my chest all day, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two weeks where these feelings were present nearly every day, without much relief, and were definitely worse than just feeling stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. Way more than two weeks. Like I was saying earlier, this whole low, flat stretch has been going on for about six months now. Every single day. It's just... persistent. Which is why I completely dropped out of my usual routines, like the art classes and those morning walks I told you about. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several weeks or more in the past, before this current six-month stretch, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble finding joy in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, several weeks... I mean, maybe? I'm trying to think back. When I was in my forties, maybe? It's so hard to separate feeling down from just being overwhelmed with life, you know? Like when my sister moved to Florida. That was a really tough adjustment. She moved to Naples, right near the water. Have you ever been to Naples? It's beautiful, but the humidity is just awful in the summer. She has this lovely little condo with a lanai—that's what they call a screened-in porch down there—and she grows orchids on it. She actually sent me an orchid for my birthday last year, a purple one. I tried so hard to keep it alive. I read all the instructions, bought the special fertilizer, only gave it three ice cubes of water a week just like the tag said. But the leaves still turned yellow and it just completely died. I felt terrible about it. I just don't have a green thumb, I guess. My mother, though, she could grow anything. She had this huge vegetable garden when I was growing up... tomatoes, zucchini, cucumbers. We'd have fresh salads all summer long. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, feeling down for a few weeks. Honestly, I really can't say for sure if I've ever had a period like that before this whole six-month thing started. Nothing really stands out as being this bad for that long.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for several weeks or more, even if it wasn't as long or as severe as this time?\n\nPatient: Several weeks... you know, I'm really trying to rack my brain here, but no, I honestly don't think so. We use these little handheld scanners now, which are supposed to make it all so much easier, but half the time the battery dies or the barcode is smudged. \n\nJust last year, Sarah actually dropped one of the scanners right into a bucket of mop water. They sent down this new regional manager last month, this young guy in a very shiny suit, who kept pacing around my floor talking about \"synergy\" and \"foot traffic metrics.\" I just looked at him and thought, you wouldn't know how to sell a greeting card if your life depended on it. \n\nBut... oh, I'm sorry, you were asking about feeling down. At least two weeks... no, I really, honestly can't say that I have. I mean, my daily life is usually so busy, especially with the store. It's hard to even find two weeks to take off for a vacation, let alone let something affect my work for that long. Getting time off approved is like pulling teeth sometimes. Poor thing was so stressed out, she started stress-baking. She brought in these lemon bars that were just out of this world. She uses extra zest in the crust, which I never would have thought to do. I usually just stick to my grandmother's recipe for pound cake if I'm baking. It calls for a whole pound of butter, which sounds terrible for you, but it makes the cake so incredibly moist. I brought one to the neighborhood block party last summer, and Mrs. Higgins from three doors down asked for the recipe. We used to take that route to go down to the shore every summer... anyway, I'm here because, well, I guess what finally scared me into making this appointment is that I’ve been having these thoughts lately. Just... thoughts like, 'If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.' I mean, I don’t want to hurt myself or anything, I've got my grandbabies to think about, and the store—I manage a little boutique downtown, and the girls there would be completely lost without me if I didn't show up to do the scheduling. But yeah, just those passing thoughts about not waking up. It just sort of spooked me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... this whole low, flat feeling, where I just can't seem to muster up the energy for anything, it's been going on for about six months now. Which is a shame, really, because six months ago was right when the weather was starting to get nice. I usually love that time of year. I'd been taking these wonderful watercolor classes at the community center—my instructor, Mrs. Gable, she always said I had such a good eye for color. We were working on landscapes, and I had this beautiful piece started of the old lighthouse down by the cove. But I just... stopped going. And my morning walks, too. I used to walk the neighborhood every single morning, rain or shine. Mrs. Higgins next door used to set her watch by me, she'd wave from her porch with her little Yorkie. But these last six months, even those things just stopped feeling restorative. I just couldn't find the point in them anymore. It's like someone just turned the color down on everything, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in them altogether?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. That's just it. Like with my art. I still set out my paints on the kitchen table—I have this lovely little setup by the window where the morning light comes in just perfectly. It's the same window where my cat, Barnaby, likes to sun himself. He's a rescue, you know, a big orange tabby. The shelter said he was unadoptable because he was too shy, but he just needed a quiet house. Anyway, I'll set out the paints, or I'll think about going to that pottery class I signed up for at the rec center, but when I get there, or when I sit down at the table, I just... stare at the materials. I just look at them and I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s just blank. Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: So far, you've mentioned a loss of interest in activities, low energy, and thoughts about not waking up. Have you also experienced a period lasting most of the day, nearly every day, where you felt persistently down or hopeless, separate from just feeling tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. They have these lovely floral prints—tulips and daffodils, mostly yellows and soft pinks. I was telling my assistant manager, Sarah, that we should do a whole window display around a garden theme. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I think he finally picked one out that her sister helped him find. But anyway, yes, that heavy feeling is just there. It just sits on my chest all day, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two weeks where these feelings were present nearly every day, without much relief, and were definitely worse than just feeling stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. Way more than two weeks. Like I was saying earlier, this whole low, flat stretch has been going on for about six months now. Every single day. It's just... persistent. Which is why I completely dropped out of my usual routines, like the art classes and those morning walks I told you about. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. It really just seems to be this one long stretch lately.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for a couple of weeks or more, even if it didn’t last as long as this time?\n\nPatient: A couple of weeks... no, I really don't think so. I've been trying to think back, but honestly, nothing comes to mind. Well, actually, a couple of weeks is exactly how long my husband and I went to Italy for our 25th anniversary. Oh, it was just magnificent. We started in Rome, and the architecture... you just can't even imagine it until you're standing right there looking at the Colosseum. We had this tour guide, Lorenzo, who was just so passionate about the history. He waved his hands around the whole time he talked. \n\nWe took the train up to Florence after that, and let me tell you, the gelato there is dangerous. I must have had a cone every single day. Stracciatella was my favorite—it's like chocolate chip, but the chocolate is in these fine little ribbons instead of chunks. I tried to find something similar at the grocery store when we got back, but that brand they sell in the plastic tubs just isn't the same. The lid on those tubs always cracks down the side anyway, and then the ice cream gets freezer burn. I ended up having to throw out a whole half-gallon of butter pecan last week because it tasted like ice crystals. My husband was so disappointed, butter pecan is his absolute favorite. He likes to put a little drizzle of caramel syrup on it... \n\nBut anyway, no, to answer your question, I really haven't ever had a couple of weeks where I felt down or lost interest in things before this whole current thing started.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt so down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, hobbies, or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: At least two weeks... no, I really, honestly can't say that I have. I mean, my daily life is usually so busy, especially with the store. It's hard to even find two weeks to take off for a vacation, let alone let something affect my work for that long. Getting time off approved is like pulling teeth sometimes. You have to submit the request through this new online portal they rolled out in January, and it's just a nightmare to navigate. The font is so small, and if you click the wrong dropdown menu, it resets the whole form. \n\nMy assistant manager, Brenda, she accidentally requested two weeks of unpaid leave instead of her vacation time, and it took payroll three whole pay cycles to fix it. Poor thing was so stressed out, she started stress-baking. She brought in these lemon bars that were just out of this world. She uses extra zest in the crust, which I never would have thought to do. I usually just stick to my grandmother's recipe for pound cake if I'm baking. It calls for a whole pound of butter, which sounds terrible for you, but it makes the cake so incredibly moist. I brought one to the neighborhood block party last summer, and Mrs. Higgins from three doors down asked for the recipe. She's the one with the two golden retrievers who are always digging up my hydrangeas... \n\nBut, oh, right, affecting my daily life for two weeks. No, doctor, really, before these last six months, I've never had anything like that happen. This is really the first time it's been this bad.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a time in your life before this current six-month period when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a row that it affected your daily life, even if it didn't last as long as this time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Two weeks in a row of feeling hopeless... you know, I really don't think so. I mean, the only time I felt truly hopeless for two weeks straight was during our kitchen remodel a few years ago. Oh, what an absolute nightmare that was. We hired this contractor, Gary, who came highly recommended by my sister-in-law, but I swear the man operated in his own personal time zone. He told us it would be a quick job, and we ended up eating microwave dinners on folding trays in the living room for nearly three months. 

The dust was just... it was everywhere. You couldn't escape it. My dog, Buster, kept tracking these powdery white drywall footprints all over the oriental rug in the dining room. I finally had to go rent one of those heavy-duty carpet cleaners from the hardware store down the street, the ones that are so clunky you can barely lift them. And of course, the teenager working the rental counter didn't even offer to help me carry it out to the trunk. Kids these days just do not have the same customer service skills we were taught. I see it at my store all the time—they just want to lean against the cash wrap and look at their phones instead of folding the display tables. I have to constantly remind them that the floor needs to be completely fronted and faced before we lock the doors... 

Anyway, I'm sorry, I know I'm rambling again. But to answer your question, no. Before this current six-month spell, I've never had a time where I felt depressed or lost my interest in things for two weeks like that. This is the first time it's ever stuck around.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks in a row... no, I really can't say that I have. I mean, the only thing I can think of that lasted two weeks was that awful sinus infection I had back in 2018. Oh, it was just miserable. I went through three boxes of tissues, and not the cheap kind either, the ones with the lotion in them. If you use the regular ones, your nose gets so red and raw you look like Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer. 

I actually tried to find those lotion tissues at the pharmacy last week, but they completely rearranged the aisles. They moved all the cold and flu stuff over by the greeting cards, which makes absolutely no sense to me. Who wants to look at birthday cards when they're sneezing their head off? And the greeting card section is always so picked over anyway. I was trying to find a nice anniversary card for my niece, Sarah, and all they had left were those silly ones with the cartoon animals that play music when you open them. Sarah is turning 30 next month, she doesn't want a singing monkey on her card. She's a lawyer now, you know, works for a very prestigious firm downtown. They make her work ridiculous hours, though. She's always answering emails during Sunday dinner...

But, oh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, feeling down for two weeks. No, doctor, before this current stretch, I really haven't ever experienced anything like that. This is definitely new for me.

I'd been taking these wonderful watercolor classes at the community center—my instructor, Mrs. Gable, she always said I had such a good eye for color. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several weeks or more in the past, before this current six-month stretch, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble finding joy in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, several weeks... It's the same window where my cat, Barnaby, likes to sun himself. He's a rescue, you know, a big orange tabby. The shelter said he was unadoptable because he was too shy, but he just needed a quiet house. Anyway, I'll set out the paints, or I'll think about going to that pottery class I signed up for at the rec center, but when I get there, or when I sit down at the table, I just... stare at the materials. I just look at them and I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s just blank. Barnaby will come rub against my leg, and usually I'd be mixing colors and talking to him, but lately I just end up putting the caps back on the tubes and making a cup of tea instead. Peppermint, usually, because the chamomile makes me too sleepy, and I already feel so tired all the time. But yes, the joy in it is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: So far, you've mentioned a loss of interest in activities, low energy, and thoughts about not waking up. Have you also experienced a period lasting most of the day, nearly every day, where you felt persistently down or hopeless, separate from just feeling tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. It's just this heavy, gray feeling that settles over me. Most mornings lately, I wake up with it, and it feels like the color has just drained out of everything before I even get dressed for the store. Speaking of the store, we just got this new shipment of spring scarves in, and usually I'd be so excited to merchandise them. They have these lovely floral prints—tulips and daffodils, mostly yellows and soft pinks. I was telling my assistant manager, Sarah, that we should do a whole window display around a garden theme. Sarah’s such a dear, she just got engaged to a boy she met in college, and she’s been showing me pictures of engagement rings on her phone for months. I think he finally picked one out that her sister helped him find. But anyway, yes, that heavy feeling is just there. It just sits on my chest all day, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two weeks where these feelings were present nearly every day, without much relief, and were definitely worse than just feeling stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. Way more than two weeks. Like I was saying earlier, this whole low, flat stretch has been going on for about six months now. Every single day. It's just... persistent. Which is why I completely dropped out of my usual routines, like the art classes and those morning walks I told you about. I used to walk down Elm Street, where they have those gorgeous old oak trees. The city was actually talking about cutting them down last year to widen the road, can you believe that? We had a whole petition going at the shop. Sarah—my assistant manager I mentioned—she actually designed the flyers for it because she’s so good with computers. She uses this program, I think it's called Canva? She made them look so professional. Anyway, we got hundreds of signatures from our regular customers and the city council finally backed down and left the trees alone. It was such a relief. But... well, even with the trees saved, I just haven't been out to walk under them. For six whole months, those walks just haven't felt restorative at all. So yes, it's definitely been longer than two weeks, and it's much worse than just being tired from work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or is this the first time you’ve experienced a low mood that lasted this long?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think it's the first time. I mean, I've had times where I felt down before, sure. Like when my husband passed, of course, that was a terrible time. But this... this feels a bit different, or maybe it's just that I'm older now. I'm 62, you know, and sometimes I think maybe it's just the age, or the retail business wearing me down. Retail has changed so much since I first started as a supervisor. It used to be about the personal connection, helping someone find exactly the right gift for their mother's birthday or picking out the perfect scarf. Now, everyone just buys things online. They come into the shop, look at the price tag, and then pull out their phones to see if they can get it cheaper on Amazon. It's really disheartening. Sarah, my assistant, she tries to tell me that we have to adapt, that we need an \"online presence.\" She even set up an Instagram account for the store. She takes pictures of the displays—like the spring scarf one I mentioned—and puts all these little hashtags on them. #ShopLocal, #BoutiqueStyle, things like that. I don't really understand how it works, but she says we've gotten a few new customers from it. But it's just not the same as having a real conversation with someone who walks through the door. So, to answer your question, I've definitely felt down before, but this six-month stretch... I'm not really sure if I've ever had it last quite this long or feel quite this heavy before. It's hard to remember, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I really don't think so. Not for six whole months. Like I said, there have been rough patches, but six months is a very long time to feel like this. It's half a year! Speaking of half a year, that's exactly how long Sarah's fiancé took to plan his proposal. He told me all about it when he came into the shop to buy her a birthday present. He got her this gorgeous silver locket we had in the display case. It was one of those vintage-style ones that opens up, and he actually brought in a tiny little picture of their golden retriever puppy to put inside it. The puppy's name is Barnaby, and he's just the sweetest thing. Sarah brings him into the shop sometimes on her days off. He has these big, floppy ears and he just plops down right in the middle of the aisle and expects everyone to rub his belly. Some of the older customers aren't too keen on having a dog in the store, but most people just love him. He did chew on the corner of one of the display tables once, though. I had to get my nephew to come over with some wood filler and stain to fix it. My nephew is so handy, he just finished remodeling his entire kitchen by himself... but anyway, no, I don't recall ever feeling this down for this long before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several weeks or more in the past, before this current six-month stretch, where you felt down, lost interest in things, or had trouble finding joy in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, several weeks... I mean, maybe? I'm trying to think back. When I was in my forties, maybe? It's so hard to separate feeling down from just being overwhelmed with life, you know? Like when my sister moved to Florida. That was a really tough adjustment. She moved to Naples, right near the water. Have you ever been to Naples? It's beautiful, but the humidity is just awful in the summer. She has this lovely little condo with a lanai—that's what they call a screened-in porch down there—and she grows orchids on it. She actually sent me an orchid for my birthday last year, a purple one. I tried so hard to keep it alive. I read all the instructions, bought the special fertilizer, only gave it three ice cubes of water a week just like the tag said. But the leaves still turned yellow and it just completely died. I felt terrible about it. I just don't have a green thumb, I guess. My mother, though, she could grow anything. She had this huge vegetable garden when I was growing up... tomatoes, zucchini, cucumbers. We'd have fresh salads all summer long. But... oh, what was I saying? Right, feeling down for a few weeks. Honestly, I really can't say for sure if I've ever had a period like that before this whole six-month thing started. Nothing really stands out as being this bad for that long.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for several weeks or more, even if it wasn't as long or as severe as this time?\n\nPatient: Several weeks... you know, I'm really trying to rack my brain here, but no, I honestly don't think so. I mean, the only thing that lasts several weeks that completely drains my joy is our annual inventory at the shop. Oh, my goodness, talk about losing interest in things! By the second week of counting every single pair of socks and tiny little silver earring, you just want to pull your hair out. We use these little handheld scanners now, which are supposed to make it all so much easier, but half the time the battery dies or the barcode is smudged. \n\nJust last year, Sarah actually dropped one of the scanners right into a bucket of mop water. The night cleaning crew had left it out—which they are absolutely not supposed to do, I've told them a hundred times, it's a terrible slipping hazard. If a customer slipped, corporate would just have a field day with us. They are always looking for an excuse to cut our store hours anyway. They sent down this new regional manager last month, this young guy in a very shiny suit, who kept pacing around my floor talking about \"synergy\" and \"foot traffic metrics.\" I just looked at him and thought, you wouldn't know how to sell a greeting card if your life depended on it. \n\nBut... oh, I'm sorry, you were asking about feeling down. No, really, I can't remember ever feeling depressed or losing my joy for a few weeks before all this started. It really just seems to be this one long stretch lately.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had a time when you felt down or lost interest in things for a couple of weeks or more, even if it didn’t last as long as this time?\n\nPatient: A couple of weeks... no, I really don't think so. Oh, it was just magnificent. We started in Rome, and the architecture... you just can't even imagine it until you're standing right there looking at the Colosseum. We had this tour guide, Lorenzo, who was just so passionate about the history. He waved his hands around the whole time he talked. \n\nWe took the train up to Florence after that, and let me tell you, the gelato there is dangerous. I must have had a cone every single day. Stracciatella was my favorite—it's like chocolate chip, but the chocolate is in these fine little ribbons instead of chunks. I tried to find something similar at the grocery store when we got back, but that brand they sell in the plastic tubs just isn't the same. The lid on those tubs always cracks down the side anyway, and then the ice cream gets freezer burn. I ended up having to throw out a whole half-gallon of butter pecan last week because it tasted like ice crystals. My husband was so disappointed, butter pecan is his absolute favorite. I have to constantly remind them that the floor needs to be completely fronted and faced before we lock the doors... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I know I'm rambling again. But to answer your question, no. We hired this contractor, Gary, who came highly recommended by my sister-in-law, but I swear the man operated in his own personal time zone. He told us it would be a quick job, and we ended up eating microwave dinners on folding trays in the living room for nearly three months. \n\nThe dust was just... it was everywhere. You couldn't escape it. My dog, Buster, kept tracking these powdery white drywall footprints all over the oriental rug in the dining room. I finally had to go rent one of those heavy-duty carpet cleaners from the hardware store down the street, the ones that are so clunky you can barely lift them. And of course, the teenager working the rental counter didn't even offer to help me carry it out to the trunk. Kids these days just do not have the same customer service skills we were taught. I see it at my store all the time—they just want to lean against the cash wrap and look at their phones instead of folding the display tables. I have to constantly remind them that the floor needs to be completely fronted and faced before we lock the doors... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I know I'm rambling again. But to answer your question, no. Before this current six-month spell, I've never had a time where I felt depressed or lost my interest in things for two weeks like that. This is the first time it's ever stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had an episode where you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Two weeks in a row... no, I really can't say that I have. I mean, the only thing I can think of that lasted two weeks was that awful sinus infection I had back in 2018. Oh, it was just miserable. I went through three boxes of tissues, and not the cheap kind either, the ones with the lotion in them. If you use the regular ones, your nose gets so red and raw you look like Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer. \n\nI actually tried to find those lotion tissues at the pharmacy last week, but they completely rearranged the aisles. They moved all the cold and flu stuff over by the greeting cards, which makes absolutely no sense to me. Who wants to look at birthday cards when they're sneezing their head off? And the greeting card section is always so picked over anyway. I was trying to find a nice anniversary card for my niece, Sarah, and all they had left were those silly ones with the cartoon animals that play music when you open them. Sarah is turning 30 next month, she doesn't want a singing monkey on her card. She's a lawyer now, you know, works for a very prestigious firm downtown. They make her work ridiculous hours, though. She's always answering emails during Sunday dinner...\n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, feeling down for two weeks. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
